#218. Women are taking pay cuts and 'white-knuckling it' to manage menopause and work: 'I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines'

Around 2020, Amy Terry noticed she didn't feel like her usual self.

The 50-year-old Michigander was working as a director of programming at a health association when she says she began experiencing symptoms like memory loss, low energy, mental processing difficulties — or, as she describes it, "waking up every day feeling like you were slogging through mud."

"I went from being this highly competent professional person to barely being able to get through phone calls," Terry says.

As the healthcare system strained under the weight of the Covid-19 pandemic, Terry, like many other people, struggled to get care. When she finally did, she found out it wasn't Covid-19. It was perimenopause.

Terry got through a few more years juggling her symptoms with her job, but by 2023, she couldn't hold out any longer. She quit to start independent consulting.

Women, and some trans and non-binary people, often experience career setbacks stemming from menopause that can hurt them financially and professionally. Four in 10 women who have experienced menopause or perimenopause say it has negatively affected their life or work, according to a recent survey of over 3,800 U.S. women from Gallup and Melinda French Gates' Pivotal, a group of organizations focused on social progress for women and young people.

Of them, roughly 28% felt limited in the effort they could give at work, 15% decided against pursuing a promotion or leadership role, 12% reduced their hours, and 10% took an extended leave from work or didn't return.

"This isn't just a health issue," says Jasmine Escalera, career expert at resume builder site LiveCareer. "It's a workplace issue."

A 2023 Mayo Clinic study estimated menopause symptoms drove $1.8 billion in lost work time per year in the U.S. alone.

Perimenopause, the transitional period before menopause, typically begins in one's 40s and can last several years, often bringing symptoms like hot flashes, sleep issues, irritability and lower energy, according to the Office on Women's Health. Menopause typically occurs in one's 50s and marks 12 months since a person's last period, but is often used colloquially to include the preceding perimenopause as well.

The timing of menopause can create a perfect storm. It frequently comes at a time in many women's careers when they're breaking into leadership or approaching their peak earning years, which is often between the ages of 35 and 44 for women, according to data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It also typically happens when many are caregiving for aging parents or raising children. This can make for an impossible choice for many women.

"Women have fought so hard for their place in the workforce," says Mimi Wied, who began perimenopause around a decade ago. "They don't want to lose it by looking like they can't handle it."

The career consequences for women "may increase the gaps we're trying to decrease," Escalera says, referring to disparities such as those in women's pay and senior-level representation relative to men. "That is a definite talent breakdown."

For Terry, becoming an independent consultant cost her employer-sponsored healthcare at the time she most needed it. It also drastically changed her income: She says she took around an 85% pay cut in the transition.

"These are supposed to be my highest-earning years," Terry says, "and I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines."

Many women are unprepared for menopause because of social stigma around discussing it, poor health education and dismissal of women's health issues.

"I've been in the healthcare environment for 30 years; if I can't figure this out, I don't know how anyone can," says Terry.

Wied also says she didn't know what to expect. The answer quickly became apparent: The 48-year-old from Brenham, Texas, says she experienced everything from night sweats and sleeplessness to anxiety and rage.

"Sometimes I didn't feel in control of my own mouth and my body and my actions," she says.

Wied left her job as a museum docent for a secretarial role paying roughly $20,000 less when she worried her irritability would affect her capacity to help visitors, she says: "If I could have quit working, I would have."

When women do scale back at work because of menopause, they may struggle to regain that professional ground later. Terry's job switch wasn't just downshifting to a part-time role: "It's an entire change in the trajectory of your career," she says.

Factors like ageism in the job search for those of menopausal age, as well as the high cost of healthcare, particularly for common menopause treatments like hormone replacement therapy, may keep some women silently struggling through their symptoms at work.

Erin Levin is currently working through perimenopause. As a high school English teacher, the 41-year-old from San Jose, California, needs to stay on her A-game because "there is no down moment in teaching," she says.

But since starting perimenopause around two years ago, she's experienced brain fog, difficulty sleeping, and heightened anxiety and stress that make her work difficult sometimes, she says. She sometimes struggles with tasks that "were not an issue" for her a few years ago.

"Now I'm just like, God, why does it just feel so exhausting all the time?" Levin says.

Her parents help with childcare some evenings, and she tries not to schedule big tasks first thing in the morning, but it's been an adjustment. Scaling back at work isn't an option financially, she says, as a single mother sharing custody of her child.

"I still have to maintain the same appearance of having all the same skills and energy level as other people who aren't in my medical position," Levin says. "I just have to white-knuckle it."

Some women see hope in coming legislation and employer benefits offerings.

Last year, Rhode Island became the first state to enact workplace protections for women experiencing menopause. In September, Sens. Kirsten Gillibrand, D-N.Y., and Rick Scott, R-Fla., held the first congressional hearing devoted to menopause. Illinois and the city of Philadelphia have also adopted legislation taking effect in 2027 providing accommodations and protections for those in menopause.

In September, a nongovernmental organization, the International Organization for Standardization, published the first standard for developing workplace policies and practices supporting people through menstruation and menopause.

Many organizations have a ways to go in providing menopause-related benefits for their employees.

In a 2026 study from the Society for Women's Health Research and workplace menopause research organization The Fuchsia Tent, fielded among 1,100 people, around one-third of decision-makers said menopause support is currently in place or actively planned in their workplace. Fewer than 10% of employees said their organization offers menopause-specific support.

Respondents said some broader benefits, like flexible scheduling, remote options, and confidential telehealth or virtual care, would also be helpful for menopause.

Terry, Wied and Levin said they had to meet with multiple doctors before finding one who'd take their concerns seriously and who'd prescribe them hormone replacement therapy.

Levin and Wied each have a daughter. They hope better support and women's healthcare are available for their daughters when they begin menopause.

"I'm hoping that by the time my daughter goes through this, we'll have a lot more information, a lot more resources, because as it stands, it makes you super angry," Wied says.

"It's a cost on my energy, it's a cost on my stress levels, it's a cost on my bank account," Levin adds. "I don't want my daughter to have to face any of that."

As for Terry, she says she was part of a study examining potential mental health impacts of hormonal fluctuations in perimenopausal women; she hopes the research can help future generations of women. Meanwhile, her switch to independent consulting has offered her greater flexibility and a more manageable workload. But it's not perfect.

"I'm worried about retirement, I'm worried about having enough money, I'm worried about being able to continue healthcare," she says. "But I also feel like I have to take the time to feel like I'm on solid ground again."

#216. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#214. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#213. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.

#211. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#210. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#209. CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

#207. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#206. Californians reject wealth tax for billionaires

California voters have voted to reject a wealth tax on its billionaires.

The ballot proposal would have levied a one-time tax on the state's mega wealthy to help fund healthcare. It took nearly a week of counting votes after the midterm election to determine a projection on the measure.

There are about 200 billionaires in the state, including Meta's Mark Zuckerberg, reality television star Kim Kardashian and media mogul Oprah Winfrey.

Proposition 40 targeted Californians with a wealth of more than $1.1bn (Â£800m) with a 5% tax on their total wealth, including assets such as shares as well as income. It failed amid divisions among Democrats and labour unions.

Are California voters about to impose 'world's first' tax on billionaires?

Had it passed, experts believe it would have been the world's first wealth tax targeting billionaires.

The tax would have raised about $100bn to make up for federal funding cuts to healthcare in California as a result of President Donald Trump's sweeping tax and spending bill.

Those who backed the measure had argued it would help ensure that the ultra-wealthy were contributing their fair share and helping backfill gaps in the state's budget.

The wealth tax faced fierce opposition, including from Google co-founder Sergey Brin, as well as California Governor Gavin Newsom and labour unions.

Newsom, whose tenure as governor is coming to an end, had expressed concerns that the measure could cause the wealthy to leave the state and drive out their crucial income tax revenue.

Some billionaire critics of the measure spent millions trying to crush it, with a handful even moving out of the state to evade the possible tax. Brin gave more than $100m to a political organisation funding ballot measures that could block the tax, according to the Los Angeles Times.

Dave Regan, head of the union that led the charge for the measure, said on election night that the billionaires' efforts to block the tax created "a lot of confusion and a lot of second-guessing among people who really in their hearts know what the right the thing to do is".

Regan, who is president of Service Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, pledged to seek a billionaire tax once more in 2028.

The measure had odds stacked against it, with two other propositions on the ballot that would have effectively reversed it, even if it passed.

California, home to some 23 million registered voters, has the largest economy in the country and boasts the fourth largest global economy. It is home to one of the highest concentrations of billionaires in the world, but also has one of the highest wealth gaps.

#204. U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

#203. How GM is trying to talk used car shoppers into buying new vehicles

Affordability challenges are pushing some buyers out of the new vehicle market. Many are choosing used cars instead.

In turn, automakers are looking for ways to compete with many of the online retailers grabbing market share in the used car market and trying to bring some of those customers back into a new vehicle.

New vehicle sales tend to be more profitable for automakers, but reselling trade-ins as used vehicles is highly profitable for dealers. Used car prices are relatively strong, but newer online retailers such as Carvana and CarMax have made the market more competitive for dealers and other incumbent used car sellers.

"The internet and technology and apps on our phone have changed every aspect of retail, and probably one of the slowest areas of retail to have adopted to online and digital shopping has been the auto industry," said Ivan Feinseth, partner, chief investment officer and director of research at Tigress Financial Partners.

General Motors is a key example of automakers' attempts to address this shifting market. The automaker has revamped its certified used vehicle program, which now operates under the CarBravo name. It's essentially an online portal through which Chevrolet, Buick and GMC dealers can list their certified used vehicles and make them available to customers all over the U.S.

About 1,615 of GM's 3,700 dealers are enrolled in the program, according to the company. The only GM brand not involved is Cadillac, which maintains its own program.

"Certified" used or "preowned" vehicles are used cars that have undergone a thorough inspection and reconditioning. They typically come with guarantees and warranties from the manufacturer.

Many automakers have certified used programs for their own cars. But GM, as part of its revamp, is using CarBravo to certify vehicles made by other automakers. A lot of those cars arrive at dealerships as trade-ins, and often dealers will sell them to wholesale auctions, said John Fitzpatrick, senior manager of the CarBravo program.

"Wholesaling doesn't bring nearly the revenue that selling it to a new customer does," he said.

GM has said it is planning to net about 250,000 sales in its CarBravo program in 2026. Fitzpatrick said he doesn't expect that even half of those will end up being new car sales.

But through CarBravo, the customer gets access to GM's dealership network for service, something Fitzpatrick says is a key differentiator between its program and growing online sellers. CarBravo vehicles come with various warranties, depending on the age and mileage on the car.

GM said the program is driving more traffic into local dealerships, where purchases are typically finalized. That creates an opportunity to convert a used car shopper into a new car buyer. So far, the automaker said about 38% of the shoppers initially looking for used cars end up buying new.

A customer who wants to buy a full-size truck can look on the website and compare used Chevrolet Silverados alongside used Ford F-150s and Ram 1500s, for example. The buyer might settle on a used truck from any of those brands, but a conversation with a dealer can push them in a different direction.

"The customer picks up the phone, but in the back of their mind, they maybe always have been a traditional, more new car customer," Fitzpatrick said.

"You've shown the customer, you're going to pay $500 [per month] for this used F-150, but you may pay $510 or $515 for this new Chevy," he said. "And now all of a sudden the customer says, yeah, maybe I can do that because I'd rather have that new vehicle than the used truck."

While the average transaction price of a new vehicle is about $50,000, the average 3-year-old used car costs about $30,000 as of September, according to Cox Automotive.

However, industry analysts say customers base their purchasing decisions on the cost of the monthly payment more than on the sticker price of the car.

New cars often come with longer-term loan options than used ones, Feinseth said, which can result in lower monthly payments.

"On a 1- or 2-year-old car, you may be only stuck with a 3- or 4-year loan," he said. "On a new car, you can get a 5-year, 6-year loan."

The share of new car loans lasting 84 months hit a new record in the third quarter, at more than 1 in 4 loans, according to Edmunds.

Meanwhile, used car prices in some affordable segments have risen, according to Cox Automotive, and about three quarters of consumers express some interest in certified cars.

But the key is building the relationship with the customer, he said. About 50% of the buyers GM has gained through the program appear to be new to the brand, meaning there is no record of them in any of the automaker's customer databases. About 70% had never visited the dealership they ended up purchasing from.

"The goal is really to get that customer engaged with the store, give them the option of building a relationship with the dealer," he said. "The dealer can really help the customer figure out what works for their budget."

It's a boon for dealers too: Customers buying warranty-backed used cars often return to the dealership for service visits — which often account for about half of a dealership's profits. GM can also sell subscriptions to connected vehicle services, such as its OnStar, which provides safety, security and navigation services.

"That is a big part of General Motors' business going forward, is to sell the software and subscription services like OnStar and eventually to the cars that are enabled with Super Cruise," GM's advanced driver-assistance system, Feinseth said.

GM isn't the only automaker trying to use its certified preowned vehicle program to lure buyers.

Detroit rival Ford Motor also certifies vehicles made by other manufacturers through its Ford Blue Advantage program. It is on track to do 450,000 total sales in 2026, more than GM's 250,000 target. It didn't specify how many of those buyers end up purchasing new vehicles.

Toyota told CNBC in an email that about 40% of its used car shoppers end up buying new instead.

Honda didn't give specifics about its program. But Lance Woelfer, American Honda's vice president of automobile sales, said conversions happen "probably every day at dealerships throughout the country."

"But it also is more about that first interaction with the consumer," Woelfer said. "And there are people in any market that prefer to buy a used vehicle, but appreciate the confidence that a [certified preowned designation] can give them."

Woelfer said those customers come back to get their vehicles serviced at a rate about 18% higher than someone who buys a non-certified used car. They also return to purchase a new car at a rate about 7% to 8% higher in the future, he said.

"It's about that first relationship that allows us to then have that customer loyalty for life," he added. "And that's where our focus is. We've been doing it a long time, and we've seen a lot of benefits from the certified business."

#202. Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang, 63, has been called the 'Taylor Swift of tech' in the US

The tech world has spent the day digesting the extraordinary news that Jensen Huang - the charismatic, leather-jacket wearing chief executive of the multi-trillion dollar firm Nvidia - is being investigated by his own company over an extramarital affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

None of us had that on our bingo cards for 2026.

Huang's alleged romantic affair with Alice Huang (no relation), the former CEO of a Singapore-based data centre company, has cast ripples far beyond his immediate family - undoubtedly on an emotional rollercoaster of their own - taking us to a crossroads at which technology, geopolitics and national security intersect.

In a nutshell, this is about the bitter rivalry between the US and China, as well as whether Jensen Huang has betrayed the US as well as his wife.

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

The US is locked in a fierce race with China to develop the best AI models.

"Whoever wins AI, wins," as US President Donald Trump puts it. He thinks that the country with the more dominant AI technology will benefit the most from it and, ultimately, will be the most powerful in the world. He may well be right.

Nvidia happens to make the best chips in the business for AI â so much so that it has been overwhelmed with demand.

It sets its prices accordingly: a single unit of the firm's latest kit for data centres starts at Â£37,000 ($50,000). A medium or large site might have hundreds of them.

The US administration initially slapped an export ban on Nvidia's more recent devices to prevent China from taking advantage of their superior capabilities. It has since softened the rules a little.

But there are suggestions that Alice Huang's company, Megaspeed, may have been involved in sneaking them into the country while she was in charge. Bloomberg reported that the firm bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia tech in November 2025. Megaspeed denies that it was used illegitimately.

For his part, Jensen Huang has lobbied heavily for the export rules between the two countries to be relaxed, arguing against the weight of general opinion that the AI race doesn't matter and that it would be no bad thing for China to become dependent on US tech.

"President Trump wants American to win and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them to do so," he told the BBC last year.

I think it's safe to assume that neither country sees it that way.

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

The difficult questions Nvidia now has to try to answer include whether Alice gained special access to the firm's latest technology, either deliberately or not, via Jensen - and, if so, whether she shared it with Beijing or others.

We don't know when their affair began or ended, but there are numerous photos and videos of Jensen and Alice together at events. It is feasible that there were moments when they spent quite a lot of time together and Jensen has talked about always having an eye on his work.

China denies that she was engaged in espionage.

Interest in economic espionage is neither new nor unique to China. But many Western countries say that China does it on a grand scale, using a combination of technology tools and agents embedded in various organisations - including private companies.

China having access to significant enough numbers of Nvidia's chips to make use of them, or stripping them down and learning how to make its own versions, would infuriate the Trump administration, which is trying to keep its AI technology close to its chest.

Some models have even been withheld from the UK-based AI Safety Institute on the grounds that they are potentially too dangerous to be shared. The US does not want anyone having a headstart or misusing its innovations.

Another potential bombshell lies in the form of any national security information to which Jensen Huang may have been privy.

He is a man operating at the very top of the US elite - one of a handful of people who could probably walk into the White House unannounced and have tea with Trump. He has influence, he has access and he has powerful friends. It's quite possible that secrets were shared with him.

Nvidia says there's no evidence so far that he passed anything on and the firm has launched an internal investigation. Huang says he will co-operate and wants to keep his job - though if nothing else, he has broken Nvidia's own code of conduct, which states that company members should not have relationships with clients.

At present, he has the backing of both the Nvidia board and Trump, who described him as "honourable" and speculated that there must have been a reason for what had transpired. In other words, he's not throwing his tech boss buddy under the bus.

Nvidia's share price has fallen but has not tanked spectacularly, suggesting shareholders are not abandoning the company under his stewardship in their droves either.

An AI industry with a weakened Nvidia would undoubtedly be a slower one.

And there isn't really a natural successor waiting in the wings to replace Jensen were he forced out.

Huang has run Nvidia for more than 30 years - to many, he is Nvidia. He has steered it through multiple crises and it was his decision for the firm to focus on AI before it was trendy.

He is known to be a demanding boss who expects staff to work long hours and to high standards. There are stories about him calling all-staff meetings to tell off individuals who had made mistakes. He has said this was to prevent others making the same errors â but you can only imagine how it felt for those on the receiving end.

While he awaits the conclusion of the investigation into his actions, Huang must hope that, despite not tolerating the mistakes of his colleagues in the past, they will be gracious about his own failings.

And he must also live with the irony that he was potentially caught out not by a sophisticated high-tech sting, but by the oldest trick in the book: a honey trap.

#199. Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Hundreds of billions of dollars have been wiped off the value of Nvidia after the US chipmaker announced an independent investigation would be carried out into its chief executive's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The tech firm saw its shares fall by about 4% by close of trade on Monday after Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair.

Nvidia's probe came after reports that the US believed the Nvidia boss had been targeted in an espionage campaign by China.

However, China has rejected the claims, with its foreign ministry saying allegations about Chinese spies were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, but a statement by the firm's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and chief executive Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report, external.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Jensen Huang admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children. He added he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation.

The Nvidia co-founder has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee last week asked Jensen Huang to appear for a closed-door briefing on his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information," congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford wrote in a letter.

"It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk."

After Nvidia's investigation was announced, other big tech bosses, including Elon Musk, came out in support of Jensen Huang to continue leading the firm.

US President Donald Trump said the Nvidia boss was an "honourable guy".

However, the news sparked a sell-off in Nvidia's shares, with about $250bn (Â£190bn) wiped of the company's market value.

While Nvidia shares fell, the US stock market opened the week on a positive note after last week's turmoil, with Nvidia's rival chipmakers benefiting.

But analysts suggested the hit to the company would be short-lived. Victoria Fernandez, chief market strategist at Crossmark Global Investments said investors would not abandon Nvidia, because the chipmaker was so important to the AI industry.

"Nvidia is the core of this AI trade, and without them, all these other pieces start to fall apart because the financing's not there," she told the BBC.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia, which is central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, with two other engineers in 1993.

Its chips were initially designed for gaming, but he decided to turn its focus on AI development, turning it into the world's second most valuable company.

A key concern for investors going forward is what would happen to Nvidia if Jensen Huang was removed from the company, as Fernandez said there was "no natural successor" to the chief executive.

Jensen Huang, unlike other founders in the tech industry, is vulnerable to any move by Nvidia's board of directors to oust him.

He owns only around 3.5% of the company, and Nvidia operates on a single class voting structure, meaning his stake would not be enough to act as a veto power over any board decision.

By contrast, many tech founders who lead their companies, including Mark Zuckerberg of Meta and Musk of Tesla and SpaceX, hold significant percentages of multiple types of stock in their companies.

Fernandez said she does not believe the board will remove Jensen Huang as chief executive, unless there is glaring evidence of wrongdoing related to national security or intellectual property, "but I bet they start bringing someone up as his number two, to have there for the future."

The modest fall in its shares mean investors believe it is unlikely he will step down or be removed as chief executive, but that the outcome of the investigation will be closely watched.

Even people who do not directly own shares in Nvidia can be affected by rises and falls in its value. Pensions or savings invested in pots which have stakes in the company through index tracking funds can be impacted, for example.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia chips have been a major focus of the AI race between the US and China and the White House has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Jensen Huang has been against restricting the sale of Nvidia's chips to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Additional reporter by Samira Hussain, North America business correspondent.

#197. Jensen Huang scandal casts doubt on Nvidia’s AI dominance — how we got here

Nvidia is facing one the biggest crises in its 33 year history, with founder Jensen Huang resigning as CEO Thursday after multiple reported affairs, including one with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. Now CFO Colette Kress has taken the helm as Nvidia's interim CEO.

The implications of Huang's affair with Alice Huang run far deeper than infidelity. That's because her ties to Nvidia customer Megaspeed put her in the middle of a sprawling effort to accumulate AI chips across Southeast Asia. And Megaspeed is under investigation for possibly diverting restricted Nvidia chips into the hands of Chinese companies.

The relationship between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang dates back to 2023, according to The New York Times. That was a "turbulent" period for Nvidia, Jensen Huang told CNBC during an interview at the time. Nvidia was in the midst of redesigning some chips to comply with the first in a series of strict export controls imposed by the U.S. government that restricted sales to China.

"The regulation is going to be just fine. We're going to be able to work through it," Huang told CNBC from Nvidia's headquarters in February 2023. The interview was for a documentary about how Huang created Nvidia and built it into the backbone of the AI boom.

That same day, CNBC also interviewed Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair.

It's not the first time Nvidia has faced an uncertain future. The company nearly went bankrupt in the early years after it was founded in 1993.

"Every company makes mistakes and I make a lot of them," Huang said in the 2023 interview. "And some of them put the company in peril."

In the early months of the AI boom, after OpenAI launched ChatGPT in November 2022, Nvidia was on the precipice of historic growth. Jensen Huang told CNBC he'd like to keep running the company for "three or four decades," as long as "I feel that I'm making a real contribution to the company."

Less than four years later, Jensen Huang is out, and we're taking a look back at our interviews with the Nvidia co-founder and other company executives as the chipmaker enters this next critical chapter. Watch the video for the full story.
