#130. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#128. Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has been summoned to testify next month before a Senate committee about his alleged affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Huang abruptly quit on Thursday as head of one of the world's most valuable companies after revelations of alleged extramarital affairs, in a stunning fall from grace that sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who is under scrutiny over exports of sensitive Nvidia AI chips to China, has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December.

Nvidia has lost $550bn (Â£415bn) in stock market value in just five days as the scandal dominated headlines.

Senator Tim Scott sent letters on Thursday to Huang and Lutnick requesting their testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," the South Carolina Republican posted on X.

"The American people deserve answers," he added.

Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily on 3 December, but also warned the panel could issue a subpoena to compel his testimony.

Three House of Representatives committees - on intelligence and foreign affairs and a panel on China - have also sent a letter to Huang summoning him for a closed-door interview on 1 December.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Lutnick is under scrutiny because unnamed officials in President Donald Trump's administration have been briefing US media that the commerce secretary stalled efforts to tighten export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

A commerce department spokesperson told US media that Lutnick had "proactively" agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee.

Huang, who has been Nvidia's public face since 1993, admitted on Sunday to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

On Wednesday, he was reported to have had a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive.

Alice Huang (no relation) worked as an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed. She has been suspected of being an intelligence asset for China, according to the New York Times. China has rejected that allegation as a smear.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December

US Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday the Department of Justice had not yet drawn any conclusions about whether Alice Huang was engaged in espionage for Beijing.

Nvidia officials have repeatedly declined to comment since Wednesday. A personal representative of Huang also declined to comment on Thursday.

According to US media, Huang's alleged second affair was with a long-time Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him within the company.

Sources have told the BBC that Nvidia employees had noticed the pair were unusually close.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Huang had been widely expected to weather the storm after the first affair became public, even as Nvidia announced it had launched an investigation into whether its CEO had been "the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

One current Nvidia staff member told the BBC that the initial reports of his infidelity were more or less brushed off by rank-and-file workers inside the company.

It wasn't until Huang's alleged affair with the Nvidia executive became public that workers started to feel "this is crazy", the person said.

Another Nvidia employee said they had been told not to speak with the press or otherwise comment on the situation in public.

Many staff have earned millions of dollars in compensation through their ability to cash out their Nvidia shares as the stock soared.

Nvidia has said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised", but pledged that its independent inquiry would look at all available information.

The company's meteoric rise had been led by Jensen Huang, who became a major booster of the AI industry, which relies on Nvidia for the powerful computer chips it manufactures.

Initially those chips were designed for computer gaming.

But as AI technology evolved in the last four years into chatbots and large language models, companies building AI tools suddenly required huge numbers of the Nvidia computer chips that could process large amounts of data.

Nvidia is now hunting a permanent replacement for Huang. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress was named on Thursday as interim CEO.

Several tech insiders that the BBC spoke with this week noted that Huang's downfall has been a major topic of conversation in San Francisco Bay area circles, an area filled with tech employees, industry executives and billionaires.

Huang has been married to Lori Huang for 40 years. Both of their children are also executives at Nvidia.

Nvidia's stock fall this week is the most significant five-day drop the company has seen since going public 27 years ago.

Its market value rose by more than 470% in the past five years, peaking just a few weeks ago. It is now down by 31% against a backdrop of wider uncertainty in the tech sector.

#125. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#124. After Jensen Huang’s exit, what top financial advisors are doing with Nvidia: Exclusive CNBC poll

Shares of Nvidia continued to slide after the market open on Friday after declining 4% on Thursday following news of co-founder Jensen Huang's resignation as chief executive, amid reports about his personal relationships and national security concerns. The news has raised questions for investors about what comes next for a company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

To gauge how wealth managers are responding, CNBC polled members of its Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council. Thirty of the 122 firms and professionals contacted responded to the online, multiple-choice flash poll, conducted between 11 a.m. and 5 p.m. EST on Nov. 12. The poll is a snapshot of respondents' views, not a measure of all financial advisors.

Most respondents, 19, said they are holding their Nvidia positions steady. Another four said they are increasing their stake, while seven said they do not own the stock. None said they were selling at the time of the poll.

"Most of our clients are in a position to hold; the reason is because it makes up a smaller percentage of an overall diversified portfolio," Winnie Sun, managing director and founding partner of Irvine, California-based Sun Group Wealth Partners, said in an interview. Sun is a member of the CNBC Financial Advisor Council.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

In open responses, several advisors wrote that they are taking a "wait-and-see" approach before changing their investment strategy with Nvidia and are telling their clients to do the same.

"Don't react to short-term headlines," wrote certified financial planner Peyton Jamison, president of Jamison Private Wealth Management in Alpharetta, Georgia. "The company's valuation is extremely attractive, and the focus should remain on its underlying fundamentals and long-term investment potential." His firm is ranked No. 78 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Asked what factors could change their approach to Nvidia, 13 respondents cited the outlook for AI demand, 11 pointed to valuation, and 10 mentioned company leadership or governance. Some said the decision depends on the client, with eight citing risk tolerance and seven mentioning portfolio concentration. Seven said they do not expect to change their approach.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results Tuesday, after the market close. In poll remarks, advisors said the company's fiscal third-quarter earnings will help determine their course of action.

Advisors said that for investors, what to do may depend on how much Nvidia stock they already own, when they expect to need the money and how comfortable they are with volatility.

"If this is a company that's going to keep you up at night and you just feel really uncomfortable with these unknown risks, then there was always the option to sell and move that capital to another stock or another portfolio," Sun said.

Nvidia exposure can also come through mutual funds and ETFs. Nvidia's large market value makes it a significant holding in many broad-market funds, including funds that track the S&P 500, as well as target-date funds in retirement-plan options.

Investors can check their Nvidia exposure by reviewing the fact sheets for the funds in their 401(k) or brokerage accounts, which typically list a fund's largest holdings. A stock's decline does not translate one-for-one into a fund's decline.

However, investors with dedicated technology, semiconductor or growth funds may have more Nvidia exposure than they realize, advisors say.

"Nvidia is a big part of virtually everyone's portfolio whether they take an active or passive index approach," wrote Andy Pratt, a partner and director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia, which is ranked No. 34 on the 2026 CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

He said he advises clients not to overreact to short-term news and to let the dust settle before making decisions.

— CNBC's Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#123. Heat pumps: How much heat does your home really need?

Floorplans. Air duct diagrams. Heat pump specifications. Most people in the market for a new heating system probably don't want to think about this level of detail.

But, perhaps surprisingly, some do want to think about those things. In extreme depth.

Every day, roughly 150 new users sign up to an app called Cool Calc, which works out the heat requirements of properties in the US.

Just over one third of those new users are not tradespeople but private homeowners, says founder and chief executive Bill Fiore.

When choosing a heating system, people can find themselves bombarded with information â how powerful a heat pump do they need, for example? It isn't always easy to take a tradesperson at their word.

"As an overall trend, we are seeing homeowners be more informed," explains Fiore. "They want to do a calculation on their own."

Cool Calc is used by tradespeople and homeowners to work out heat loss

A heat loss calculation is a figure, usually measured in watts, that defines how much heat a home loses during cold weather. It's worked out room by room and is a key part of designing an efficient heating system.

This estimate is especially important to get right when installing a heat pump, which is sized fairly precisely, according to the projected heat loss.

Should that calculation be off, the heat pump will likely perform poorly. You could be left with an underpowered device that struggles to warm your property, for example. Given the costs involved, homeowners are often anxious to ensure installers avoid such mistakes.

Certain new technologies are not, for the moment, helping, adds Fiore. "People are going to AI and they're getting a lot of misinformation because it's not backed by anything credible," he says. Cool Calc is planning to integrate with AI chatbots to try and improve the quality of the advice they spew out.

It is probably a good idea to be cautious about your new heating system's design, says Kevin Kircher at Purdue University. He and colleagues recently conducted a study, yet to be published, examining the heat loss calculations provided by tradespeople to roughly 75 US homeowners.

"We were surprised," says Kircher. "The heat loss calculations often led to overestimating the design heat loads by a factor of two or more." That would result in oversized systems that use much more power than necessary.

Not only that, some people reported charges of more than $1,000 (Â£745) â and in one case as much as $2,500 â just for a heat loss survey, adds Kircher.

Besides using excessive amounts of energy, an oversized heating system can also feel uncomfortable, says Dan Dempsey, a former senior engineering fellow for a major heating and cooling provider in the US.

Dempsey, who took part in Kircher and his colleagues' study, lives in Indiana. He says it is his retirement hobby to monitor the performance of heating and cooling systems in the homes of friends and family members, by analysing data from their smart thermostats.

"I spent five years manually downloading everything, every week," he says. He points out that oversized systems tend to cycle a lot, meaning they switch on and off again in quick succession.

This leads to discernible swings in temperature: rooms that fail to remain at their target temperature of, say, 20C, but rather fluctuate, which feels uncomfortable, says Dempsey.

For people seeking a better understanding of their home's heat loss, various free or partially free apps, including Cool Calc and AutoHVAC, are available. There are options for UK homeowners, too, such as web app Heatpunk.

Heat loss apps help drive business to renewable energy firms says Andy Rankin

Many things can influence the heat loss figure, including the amount of insulation in your home, whether the building has double or triple glazing, and also the size of your radiators.

Heatpunk allows people to draw a floor plan of their home and input data about the property in order to estimate its performance. Thousands of people use it every month, mostly professional installers, says Andy Rankin, founder and managing director of Midsummer Energy.

The web app also suggests specific heat pump models for each property, based on the results of the calculation. Naturally, these are models available through Midsummer Energy. "It's been a very effective marketing tool," says Rankin.

Dwellow, another company that offers heat pump installations in the UK, is keen to follow suit. The firm recently launched its own heat loss estimator.

While professional heat loss calculations remain essential, getting people to do their own "self surveys" first could ultimately reduce how much time professional heating engineers must spend analysing properties says Ian Mclaren, Dwellow's co-founder and director.

"Going into a home and just being able to check the information rather than start from scratch could turn a survey into a half-hour trip â far more efficient and less costly," says Mclaren.

There's no substitute for engineers doing a good job when it is their turn, however, stresses Maurizio Sibilla, of the Centre for Advanced Built Environment Research at the University of West England, Bristol.

"The solution is better installer practice, clearer customer-facing evidence, and improved methods, not expecting homeowners to do the engineering themselves," he says, adding that providing homeowners with information about the calculation and also data on heat pump performance post-installation can reassure them that the system works as expected.

Adam Chapman questions whether homeowners should do their own heat loss survey

Doing your own heat loss calculation may end up "over-intellectualising" things, says Adam Chapman, chief geek at heat pump training firm and installer network Heat Geek.

"I don't personally see what a homeowner would gain from it," he says. "We will guarantee the efficiency with way less radiators and pipework upgrades than any other system out there."

Chapman points out that only the most careful of surveys will capture a home's heat loss well.

He says it is common for Heat Geek installers to discover retrofitted windows that haven't been foam-insulated into their openings, leaving draughty gaps, or cavity walls that a homeowner thought had been insulated â but which were actually missed by the builder.

"It's stuff you can't see," he says. "What we actually need is home surveys that go and spot for these sorts of things."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

#119. The rise of 'housefishing' - when does using AI go from enhancing to misleading?

The images Olivia Nicole saw of a home on Rightmove (l) were completely different to how the property looked in real life (r)

Have you ever been impressed by the pictures of a property on an estate agent's website, only to find the place looks completely different when you view it in person?

Then there's a chance that AI was used to enhance the images you saw.

From repainted walls to imaginary lawns, estate agents are increasingly using the technology to make homes seem more appealing, arguing it helps buyers visualise the potential of a place.

But experts warn it can go too far and mislead prospective buyers - a trend dubbed "housefishing".

Earlier this year Olivia Nicole, 29, was searching online for five-bedroom properties in south-west London when a house on Rightmove caught her eye.

But when she arrived to view it several days later she initially thought she had come to the wrong place.

"The rooms were much smaller than they were in the pictures and the decor was totally different," she tells the BBC. "Some structural features were also different."

For example, there was a raised section of the wall behind the sofa that didn't appear in the images. The windows were also noticeably smaller. Olivia was so surprised she took her own pictures of the property for comparison.

AI has been used to make the property look more luxurious

It later turned out the pictures she had seen on Rightmove, which also appeared on the Zoopla and Winkworth websites, were enhanced with AI.

But that wasn't clear in the listing, Olivia says, and she feels the agent wasted her time.

"It was frustrating, because I had taken several hours off work for the viewing which I had to make up for."

All three companies told the BBC they had strict guidelines on how AI could be used on their websites and have since taken the images down.

Estate agents have long used wide-angle lens cameras or Photoshop to make homes seem bigger or more appealing to buyers, but AI is making this process much easier and more accessible.

It is difficult to quantify how common the practice is, says the Guild of Property Professionals, a trade group of independent estate agents, but it has issued guidance on how to use AI enhancement responsibly.

According to a recent survey, external of 906 UK homebuyers, one in five who had who purchased a home in the past year said they had viewed a property where the listing photos had been digitally enhanced to make the home appear better than it actually was.

Jack Bushrod reviews quirky property listings he finds on Rightmove for his social media channel, Housing Horrors, and says he has seen a "surge" of AI-enhanced images over the last six months.

He thinks using AI is fine up to a point - to declutter a busy room, or add furniture to an empty one so a prospective buyer can see its potential.

But he draws the line at anything that misleads a prospective buyer or wastes their time.

"I've seen one house where they had removed a pillar and added a window. Others might use AI to remove stains on the carpet or cracks in tiles. It's little details you would only notice in person."

He says most of the enhanced images he's seen weren't clearly labelled as AI-generated.

Cara says AI was used to empty her son's room in the image on the left

Cara, 38, is currently selling her home in Lowestoft, Suffolk, and says she was surprised when she saw the AI-enhanced pictures of her property her estate agent, Howards, put on Rightmove.

In one photo, her son's room was completely emptied of toys and clutter.

In another, of her daughter's room, the agent appears to have removed a doll's house, brightened the room, and used AI to change the shape of a rug, but that image has since been taken down.

Cara has had several viewings so far and says that one person has commented on the differences. But she says she trusts Howards, which has acknowledged the use of AI in the property listing.

"If they'd changed something structurally or it was really wild and didn't look like our home at all then I would put my foot down," she says.

In one picture (l), the AI removed a doll's house and changed the shape of a rug

A spokeswoman told the BBC that Howards had now introduced a policy of labelling all AI-enhanced photos. She added that the firm never used AI "to misrepresent the fundamental features of a property".

"It's about helping people imagine the possibilities, not changing the reality of what's being sold."

Trading Standards told the BBC that estate agents can use AI to make basic enhancements to photos, or to virtually furnish an empty room or garden, as long as they indicate they have used the technology.

But it says structurally changing a room, for example by adding a window, or hiding features could mislead consumers and break the law.

Jack Bushrod thinks all estate agents should have to clearly label AI-enhanced photos and mention any use of the tech prominently in the key features slide of a property listing.

He also expects to see more housefishing as time goes on.

"My worry is the more estate agents use AI, others will see the trend and jump on the bandwagon."

#117. Lutnick faces questions over Nvidia spy scandal. His sons’ firm has deep ties to the chipmaker

In late September, Cantor Fitzgerald hosted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and other top executives from the chipmaker for investor meetings in New York, according to a Cantor research report reviewed by CNBC.

The Wall Street firm liked what it heard.

"We continue to view NVDA as the best risk/reward in our coverage," Cantor analyst C.J. Muse and his colleagues wrote in a research report dated Oct. 3 that maintained a $350 price target and overweight rating on the stock.

Yet the firm formerly run by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and now led by his sons, Brandon and Kyle, reported a nearly 50% decline in the number of Nvidia shares it held between September 2025 and March 2026, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings that excludes options. Nvidia had been one of the largest stock positions reported in Cantor's filings.

The decline coincided with White House and U.S. intelligence officials pressing Lutnick to crack down on Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of funneling Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips to China. Lutnick repeatedly resisted those efforts, according to Axios.

By the time Cantor hosted Huang in New York, Lutnick had also received a classified intelligence warning, according to Axios, about Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy and had appeared alongside the CEO at a G20 technology summit in North Carolina.

Even as Cantor pared back its Nvidia stake, it continued doing business across the chipmaker's AI empire. A CNBC review of securities filings, corporate announcements and Wall Street research found the firm has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners while maintaining close ties to the chipmaker's leadership.

Those ties have drawn fresh scrutiny following Huang's resignation Thursday and Axios' reporting that Lutnick repeatedly resisted a crackdown on companies suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, despite President Donald Trump authorizing new export restrictions on those companies for a third time on Nov. 5.

An ethics expert said the relationship between Lutnick and his former firm's investments may raise questions, given what the secretary knew about Nvidia.

Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer for President George W. Bush and now a corporate law professor at the University of Minnesota said that while "there's no proof of" Lutnick sharing nonpublic information with Cantor Fitzgerald, "the appearance doesn't look great."

"His conversations with his sons better be about publicly available information," Painter told CNBC.

There is no evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance knowledge of the intelligence concerns, that changes in Cantor's reported holdings were tied to any warnings or that the firm's business interests influenced their father's decisions.

"Cantor has to date from time to time had only immaterial stand-alone proprietary positions in Nvidia, unlike the positions reflected in 13F filings which were entered into in the ordinary course of business in connection with client swap transactions, that are common in institutional markets," a Cantor spokesperson told CNBC.

Cantor declined to answer CNBC's other questions. The Commerce Department, the White House, Howard Lutnick and his sons did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment or detailed questions about the findings.

As scrutiny of Huang intensified, Cantor Chairman Brandon Lutnick was publicly praising the chipmaker's leadership. "Nvidia is one of the great companies on the planet, and they have an amazing leadership team," Brandon Lutnick told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Trump is standing by Howard Lutnick. A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in the commerce secretary.

But cracks in the administration's unity emerged Friday, when Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that the Departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months."

Bessent pointed to Commerce as the department responsible for imposing the entity list, which identifies restricted businesses and other groups, adding, "You will have to ask them about their internal processes."

Nvidia was Cantor's second-largest reported stock position before Howard Lutnick left Wall Street to become commerce secretary in February 2025, behind only bitcoin investor MicroStrategy, now known as Strategy. At the end of 2024, the firm disclosed more than 3.5 million Nvidia shares worth $479 million, according to its quarterly securities filing.

That share count held steady through September 2025, but its value climbed 36% to $652 million, according to Cantor's quarterly securities filing, as the Trump administration moved over the summer to ease restrictions on Nvidia's H20 chip sales to China.

Over the following six months, however, the number of Nvidia shares Cantor reported holding fell by 49%, to 3 million in December and 1.78 million by the end of March 2026, with their value dropping from $652 million to $310 million, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings.

Pressure on Howard Lutnick was also mounting on Capitol Hill. Sens. Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., in March urged the secretary to suspend or reconsider Nvidia's export licenses after three people linked to Nvidia customer Super Micro Computer were indicted on charges of conspiring to smuggle AI chips to China.

"The Trump administration, I think, leaks like a sieve when it comes to non-public government information getting to various hedge fund guys," Painter said, though he stressed there is no evidence Howard Lutnick had shared such information with his sons.

The filings do not establish why Cantor's reported share count fell or whether its overall financial exposure to Nvidia declined. Nvidia’s stock had more than doubled from its April 2025 lows by late October, before retreating in the following months. The stock fell about 6.5% between the end of September and the end of March.

Cantor's reported Nvidia holdings remained near that lower level through June 30, the latest quarter covered by public disclosures, at 1.81 million shares. Its next quarterly filing is due Monday.

Cantor has not publicly explained the decline.

Most of those shares were attributed to CF Secured, a Cantor brokerage subsidiary that uses stocks and derivatives to hedge customer transactions, according to its audited financial statements. Cantor also disclosed substantial Nvidia options positions during the time period CNBC examined, overwhelmingly skewed toward put options, which can protect against declines in the stock price.

As concerns about Huang escalated inside the Trump administration in May, according to Axios, Cantor was helping finance the expansion of one of Nvidia's newest business partners, securities filings show.

On May 7, Nvidia and Australia-based cloud computing provider IREN announced a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud computing contract and a strategic partnership giving the chipmaker the right to invest up to $2.1 billion in IREN.

"That's not a passive financial investment. Nvidia is a partner who wins as we deliver," IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told investors on the company's quarterly earnings call that day.

Five days later, as Trump headed to China aboard Air Force One, Secretary of State Marco Rubio warned him about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported on Tuesday. Huang, who had not initially been invited on the trip, joined the president's delegation during a refueling stop in Alaska.

Howard Lutnick had already been sent a highly classified intelligence memo detailing concerns about Huang's relationship, Axios reported.

On May 12, the day Trump departed for China, IREN announced the pricing of a convertible bond offering that ultimately raised $3 billion when it closed two days later. Cantor was one of seven bookrunners on the deal, alongside JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

On the day the offering closed, May 14, Lutnick's Commerce Department quietly cleared roughly 10 Chinese companies, including Alibaba and ByteDance, to buy Nvidia's advanced H200 chips, though some of those approvals predated the intelligence memo.

It is unclear when IREN hired Cantor for the offering or how much the firm earned from the deal. Cantor declined to answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

Still, Cantor reported a substantial position in IREN's stock.

By the end of June, Cantor reported $115 million in IREN shares and $362 million in Nvidia shares. Together, the two companies accounted for more than 9% of the value of Cantor's reported $5.2 billion in securities holdings, excluding options, according to CNBC's analysis of the filing.

Cantor didn't answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

IREN was not the only company in Nvidia's orbit that turned to Cantor for financing.

In October 2025, Cantor advised AI infrastructure developer Lancium on a $600 million financing deal to support its data center campus in Abilene, Texas, the first site of Stargate, the massive AI infrastructure project backed by OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle.

Newmark, a commercial real estate firm controlled by Cantor, also helped finance the Abilene campus, arranging a $7.1 billion construction loan in May 2025.

Ten months later, Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion for a roughly 20% stake in Lancium, with up to $1 billion more tied to future milestones, The Information reported in August. Lancium, which is also backed by Blackstone, confirmed an investment and a technology partnership on Aug. 24 without disclosing financial terms.

It's unclear whether Cantor continued advising Lancium after the financing deal or played a role in Nvidia's investment. Neither Cantor nor Lancium responded to questions about the investment.

Cantor's reported ties also extend to Nvidia's supply chain.

In May 2025, days after Howard Lutnick announced the transfer of his ownership stake in Cantor to his children via trusts, Semafor reported that bankers at the firm had informally advised executives at Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co., which makes Nvidia's most advanced chips, on navigating Washington. Cantor called Semafor's report "baseless and false."

TSMC received billions of dollars in federal subsidies created during the Biden administration and administered by the Commerce Department to fund its U.S. manufacturing expansion.

Democratic senators called for an ethics investigation in December into whether the Lutnick family stood to benefit from his push to expand AI data centers.

And on Wednesday, Sens. Warren and Chris Coons, D-Del., demanded that Howard Lutnick, Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller preserve records on Nvidia's chip exports to China and intelligence warnings about Huang.

The senators questioned whether "prudent defensive counter-intelligence action was taken or national security recommendations were overruled."

"The place where I think the Trump administration has most failed here is in being persuaded by Jensen Huang and others to license the export of some advanced chips," Coons said Friday on CNBC's "Squawk Box," accusing the administration of turning a blind eye to the black market for more advanced chips.

#116. Pressure grows on Trump's Commerce Secretary Lutnick in fallout from Nvidia scandal

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has blamed Howard Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-spec AI chips, as the fallout from the Nvidia scandal widens to President Donald Trump's cabinet.

Pressure is building on Lutnick from across the political spectrum to explain his department's actions.

Jensen Huang was ousted as Nvidia CEO after revelations he had an affair with a businesswoman who was also allegedly a Chinese spy.

Megaspeed, the firm she worked for, had been probed by US government agencies over suspicions it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China, reports said, but it is not on a list of firms US companies are prevented from working with.

Bessent told CNBC several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

Bessent's comments come after Lutnick was called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over the Commerce Department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Huang.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said some parts of the administration wanted more controls on trade with Megaspeed

Bessent said he was "frustrated" by delays in putting the company on the export control list and reports of leakage of AI chips into China.

"President Trump made it very clear that China should not receive any Nvidia chips above H100s, so any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts our great companies in the AI race, because it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Last year, the Commerce Department had been investigating Megaspeed over suspicions it had been smuggling some of Nvidia's advanced AI chips into China, according to the New York Times. Megaspeed has been contacted for comment.

There was also pressure on the Commerce Department to add it to the list, but separate reporting in the Financial Times said the department was delaying efforts to have the cloud computing firm included.

However, Bessent also pointed the finger at Nvidia.

"The story has been thus far of Jensen Huang's personal foibles," he told CNBC's Squawk Box programme, external.

"The real story here should be: Did Nvidia know their client, and was there a robust effort at Nvidia to make sure that there was no leakage and that they were selling to what were obviously pass-through shell companies?

"It's important that anyone at the company there be held accountable for that."

Bessent's pointed comments are a rare instance of internal debate within the Trump administration spilling out into the public view, although tensions have reportedly simmered between Bessent and Lutnick in the past.

In 2024, the two reportedly jostled for the role of treasury secretary ahead of Trump's return to the White House, and last year the pair butted heads over the administration's tariff policy, accoprding to US media.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Huang has been summoned to testify before the Senate Banking Committee about his alleged affair with the former Megaspeed executive, who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

The commerce secretary has also been summoned to appear before the committee in December.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," committee chairman, Republican Senator Tim Scott posted on X.

Democrats on key congressional committees have asked the Commerce Department and Lutnick to keep all records relating to decisions to allow Nvidia to sell its advanced AI chips to Chinese companies, including all their communications with or about Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Ways and Means Committee Ranking Member Richard E Neal and Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden are starting investigations into the way Nvidia was allowed to sell these chips.

According to Neal and Wyden, Nvidia's advanced H200 chips could be sold to Chinese firms if they were first imported from their manufacturing facility in Taiwan into America for inspection.

A 25% tariff - a levy that is paid to the federal government - was imposed on those chips, under a section of the Trade Act that Neal and Wyden say is meant to allow the US president to take action against imports that risk national security.

"Trump's use of the statute appears to have done the opposite, undermining US national security and putting Americans at risk," they said.

#114. Retirees sometimes tap savings for one-off expenses. Here’s what advisors say to consider first

Retirees facing a large expense that's not in their regular household budget may turn to their traditional 401(k) or individual retirement account balances to cover it.

While retirement savings may be the easiest place to turn for the money, financial advisors say there are a few things worth considering before making one-off withdrawals.

"Be aware of the fact that it's not as simple as just taking out the amount of money that [covers] what you want it for," said certified financial planner Victoria Trumbower, managing member of Trumbower Financial Advisors in Bethesda, Maryland. The firm ranked No. 19 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 list for 2026.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

At the same time, research shows that many retirees aren't even spending their nest egg. About a third still have 100% or more of their initial assets when they reach their mid-80s, according to a May study by the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonpartisan research group.

"Retirement savings are meant to be spent," said CFP Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth and a partner with Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon. "A one-time withdrawal isn't necessarily a bad decision, as long as it fits within a sustainable retirement plan."

In 2025, about 15% of 401(k) owners ages 60 to 72 with balances of at least $100,000 who had left their employer made an ad hoc withdrawal from their account, according to Vanguard's How America Retires 2026 report, which analyzed more than 125,000 ex-plan participants.

This age group falls between the onset of penalty-free withdrawals — age 59½ — and the threshold for starting required minimum distributions — currently age 73. RMDs are amounts that retirees must withdraw annually from traditional 401(k)s and IRAs.

Not all 401(k) plans allow occasional withdrawals from ex-employees — about 43% did in 2025, up from 16% in 2015, the Vanguard study says. If a plan doesn't offer such ad hoc distributions, a former employee who wants to tap that money would have to withdraw or roll over the entire balance. Typically, they roll it over to an IRA and take withdrawals from there.

The most common reasons for retirees taking irregular distributions, based on a 2025 Vanguard survey of 8,000 clients noted in the recent report, are paying off debt, with 31% of respondents citing it; covering unforeseen expenses such as medical care and home repairs, noted by 23%; and discretionary spending, with 22% citing it as a reason.

Regardless of why you take the money, advisors say a key consideration is the impact on your tax situation.

Distributions from a traditional 401(k) or IRA are generally taxed as ordinary income. If you don't have other means to cover the tax bill, you may have to withdraw extra from your retirement account to compensate — which creates a tricky situation.

"It's like a double whammy because you have to pay income tax, but you don't have any money to pay income tax, so you've got to withdraw more money to pay the tax, and then you've got to pay taxes" on the extra amount, Trumbower said.

For illustration: If you need $50,000 after taxes and assume a combined federal and state income tax rate of 25%, the math says you would need to withdraw about $66,667. That's because the money withdrawn to cover the tax bill is itself taxable. A $66,667 withdrawal taxed at 25% would result in about $16,667 in taxes, leaving $50,000 after tax.

"It's going to cost you a lot more than you think," Trumbower said.

A taxable withdrawal from retirement savings can also trigger additional costs elsewhere.

For example, if you're receiving Social Security retirement benefits, the added income could cause a larger portion of that money to become taxable. Depending on how much other income you have, 0%, up to 50%, or up to 85% of your Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income tax. A large retirement-account withdrawal can increase that other income and make more of your benefits taxable.

For retirees on Medicare, a large taxable withdrawal also could increase their Part B and Part D premiums through the income-related monthly adjustment amount, or IRMAA.

For 2026, that surcharge applies when modified adjusted gross income exceeds $109,000 for an individual filer or $218,000 for a married couple filing jointly. Medicare generally bases the surcharge on tax information from two years earlier, meaning 2026 premiums are typically based on 2024 income.

"What catches many people off guard is that they often don't feel that impact until a couple of years after the withdrawal," said CFP Ciano Villaquiran, a partner with CURO Financial Planning in San Diego.

For some retirees, it may be worth delaying the additional withdrawal needed to cover the tax bill until the next calendar year, Trumbower said. That second distribution would be taxable in the following year, potentially spreading the income — and its tax impact — across two tax years.

"Pushing the [additional] distribution from the IRA into the following calendar year may possibly avoid jumping up into a higher tax bracket," Trumbower said.

However, she said, retirees should still withhold enough tax from the initial distribution to avoid an underpayment penalty. The federal income tax system is generally pay-as-you-go, meaning taxpayers must pay enough tax through withholding or estimated payments during the year rather than waiting until they file their return.

Generally, taxpayers can avoid an underpayment penalty if their withholding and estimated payments equal at least 90% of the current year's tax liability or 100% of the prior year's tax liability. For certain higher-income taxpayers, the prior-year threshold is 110%. Any remaining tax due generally can be paid when the return is filed.

If you have a brokerage account, that may be a better source to fund a one-off expense, Trumbower said.

Taxes on long-term capital gains — profits on investments such as stocks held for more than one year — are generally lower than ordinary income tax rates. Depending on your taxable income, some long-term capital gains may even be taxed at 0%.

For 2026, the 0% long-term capital gains rate applies to taxable income of up to $49,450 for single filers and $98,900 for married couples filing jointly. Above those thresholds, most long-term capital gains are taxed at 15%, with a 20% rate applying at higher income levels.

Depending on the expense, retirees may be able to borrow money instead of tapping their savings, Trumbower said.

For example, a home equity loan or line of credit could help pay for a major home improvement project, while a car purchase could be financed with an auto loan rather than a large withdrawal from retirement savings.

Of course, borrowing comes with its own costs and risks. The loan payments would need to fit within the retiree's budget, and the interest paid could outweigh the potential benefits of leaving retirement savings untouched.

Additionally, it's worth considering how the withdrawal could affect your savings over time.

"Pulling a large amount out for a one-time expense doesn't just reduce the account balance today; it can reduce what that money could have grown into down the road," Villaquiran said.

For illustration: $50,000 earning 5% annually would grow to about $81,400 after 10 years, according to the Securities and Exchange Commission's Investor.gov compound-interest calculator. If a retiree withdrew $10,000 at the start and left $40,000 invested under the same assumptions, it would grow to about $65,200 — a difference of roughly $16,300 after 10 years.

CNBC receives no compensation from placing financial advisory firms on our Financial Advisor 100 list. Additionally, a firm's or advisor's appearance in our ranking does not constitute an individual endorsement by CNBC of any firm or advisor.

#113. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

#111. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#109. Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Watch: What we know so far about the Microsoft outage

A major Microsoft outage has impacted schools, healthcare systems, businesses, courts and governments around the world.

Services including Teams, Outlook and AI software Copilot, are being restored almost 24 hours since issues first surfaced.

Microsoft said the outage was not a cyber-security incident and was caused by a technical issue. It has apologised for the disruption.

Here's what we know about the services affected and what went wrong.

Schools, GPs and businesses hit by global Microsoft outage as company scrambles to solve issue

Some Microsoft 365 services have been affected by "connectivity problems" with problems reported in at least 74 countries.

From late on Thursday, customers reported varying levels of access - many were unable to send or receive emails, view certain files or details on their calendar apps.

But some users were able to use Teams, messaging and online meeting functions without issues.

As of Friday afternoon, Microsoft said it had seen "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe and improvement in North America.

The company added that it was focused on "addressing the remaining areas of disruption" but warned "some users may still experience intermittent issues".

In a later statement, the company said it was continuing to see improvements "with email queues significantly reduced and approaching normal levels".

The company attributed the ongoing outage to an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade.

"We have addressed the underlying issue and continue to restore service to remaining customers and regions," it said on Friday.

Although services are beginning to resume, Microsoft has warned of continued "intermittent access issues", meaning some users will still struggle to load their inboxes and could experience "delayed email delivery".

In other words, it is not quite safe to assume every email you send is going to arrive in its intended recipient's inbox right away.

Earlier, Microsoft said it would be extending the "timeout window" to 48 hours "to help protect delayed messages as delivery catches up".

The email timeout window refers to the amount of time Outlook will wait to receive an email.

When they do show up, the timestamp will be when they were delivered rather than when they were sent.

Although email service Outlook appears to be resuming, the outage has so far lasted for more than 20 hours - in outage terms that is considered a long time.

Prof James Ferryman from the University of Reading's department of computer science said the details the company has disclosed provide some clues.

"What Microsoft has told us is that the part of its servers that sets up secure connections is getting swamped, and once that happens the server just stops answering," he said.

"The trouble is that this kind of failure snowballs.

"One server falls over, its work gets passed to the next one, and meanwhile huge numbers of phones and laptops keep trying to get back in.

"You can't just turn it all back on at once. You have to bring it back bit by bit."

The challenge is therefore not just fixing the fault, but managing the huge surge of devices trying to reconnect once services start coming back online.

Gergely Orosz, a former leading software engineer at several major tech companies including Skype and Uber, said this could be the "most disruptive" business email outage "of all time".

He said it was still difficult to know just how many people have been affected by the disruption, but added he "cannot recall" an outage over business email ever taking this long to return to normal.

Australian cyber-security expert Troy Hunt told the BBC the outage could have far-reaching consequences.

"Microsoft is a massive part of the internet backbone and reminds us how critical email still is.

"Not only does this outage affect many people's ability to work but also things like the ability to communicate about appointments, purchase things that need verification, create or access accounts and services," he said.

In a statement, Microsoft said it was "deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously. We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services."

More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps according to Microsoft's website. The full scale of the outage has not been confirmed.

Microsoft 365 is used by companies worldwide and is expected to have a knock-on effect to other services.

Tech giants OpenAI and Anthropic both earlier reported problems as a result of the outage.

ChatGPT-maker OpenAI said it was investigating an issue affecting its connections to Microsoft's services, which had caused some tools to not work as expected.

From city governments in the US to Australian spy agencies, disruption across the globe has been widespread.

Courts from Los Angeles to Texas earlier warned that communications might be delayed.

In Canada, the public broadcaster CBC reported that a healthcare network running several hospitals in Toronto was experiencing email issues, but that patient care was not affected.

The government of Japan also said its shared IT platform had been disrupted.

Across the UK, GP surgeries, pharmacies, police and schools have been affected by the outage.

Some GP surgeries had urged patients not to submit messages via their website while police forces were also earlier asking the public to call if they want to make a report.

#107. Hassett denies report he's leaving White House: 'Not true'

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett on Friday poured cold water on a report that he's expected to leave the White House in the coming weeks.

That report is "not true," Hassett told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a text message.

Semafor reported earlier Friday afternoon that Hassett "has been inquiring about roles outside the administration," even before Republicans' drubbing in last week's midterm election.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, and his counselor David Zervos, are among the names being floated to replace Hassett, according to Semafor, which cited unnamed people familiar with his plans.

But a White House official told CNBC in a statement later Friday, "There is no factual basis for this reporting."

"Kevin Hassett is a key member of President Trump's economic team and will continue working to advance President Trump's economic policies," the official said on condition of anonymity to discuss the matter.

Zervos, reached by phone after Semafor's story published, declined to comment.

While Hassett denies he is departing the White House, several other Trump administration officials have stepped down since Democrats' staggering wins in the Nov. 3 midterm, flipping Congress blue and overhauling President Donald Trump's plans for the rest of his term.

White House chief of staff Susie Wiles left her role Friday and was succeeded by Stephen Miller. Multiple Department of Justice officials have also left since Election Day, including Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, and John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division.

#106. Microsoft 'deeply sorry' for global outage after almost 24 hours of disruption

Products such as Microsoft Teams and Outlook have been out of service for much of Friday

Microsoft has said it is "deeply sorry" for the service outage to its products, including Outlook and Teams, which has caused disruption around the world.

Services were broadly recovering by Friday afternoon, nearly 24 hours after issues began. Microsoft said it was making "encouraging progress", but added it does not know when services will fully recover.

The outage hit more than 70 countries, disrupting everything from business operations to government services and personal users. More than 430 million people use the affected products worldwide.

Microsoft said it was a technical issue introduced during a "network capacity upgrade" and not related to cyber or artificial intelligence agents.

Experts said the event was a "stark reminder" of how heavily businesses and vital services rely on a small group of big tech providers.

Users began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products on Thursday.

By Friday, the far-reaching issue was affecting institutions from the Japanese government and the Philippines Supreme Court to the Australian spy agency, and trains, hospitals and courts in North America.

Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Courts, spies and MPs: How the Microsoft outage has been felt around the world

In the UK, some parts of the NHS, including GPs and hospitals, were affected, as were some schools and police forces.

UK cyber minister Liz Lloyd said the incident had "limited" impact on critical frontline services such as 999 calls, and there had been no disruption to the transport network.

In Canada, however, the national rail network said passengers may not be able to receive email updates about their trips, and hospitals across the country also had issues with emails.

In the US, staff members from the House of Representatives told Politico it had set back an entire day of legislative work as everything was on Microsoft's Outlook.

Singapore's government acknowledged some of its systems had been impacted, and in India, the outage disrupted work for many on Friday.

In Turkey, advisers and officials attending the COP31 climate summit in Antalya were left frustrated by being unable to access Outlook.

The issues also affected smaller businesses and individuals.

Pete says his business has been brought to a standstill

Peter Pentz, a winemaker in South Africa, said his entire business was basically brought to a "standstill".

His winery's communication with exports was also brought to a standstill, he said, and "critical production work" was been halted as the Cape West Coast winery "relies on these systems for parts of our day-to-day operations".

Georgia from Staffordshire in England said she was due to complete her first house purchase today after waiting eight months to get to this point - but the outage has delayed that.

"The solicitor mentioned there may be a delay due to not being able to contact other agencies.

"We're not the only one completing today, there are hundreds of houses and for them to ring everyone in time is unfeasible," Georgia said.

Microsoft first acknowledged users in the Americas were unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes at 17:54 GMT on Thursday, and shortly after warned the disruption was possibly affecting Microsoft 365 globally.

While it launched an investigation and engineers began working to restore services, by 04:36 on Friday Microsoft was reporting only a "partial recovery" in some parts of the world.

By 13:32, the tech giant said there were "broader improvements" in North America, "sustained improvements" across much of Europe and "encouraging recovery signals" in the Asia Pacific region.

Microsoft said it restored services gradually due to the scale of the problem and to help ensure services remain stable once they are up and running again.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners," a Microsoft spokesperson said.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

Microsoft said it has no "ETA" for when services will be fully recovered.

A full post-incident report will be released once Microsoft has completed a review.

Experts said while workers may have been happy to not have email access for the day, the incident showed the dependence many organisations have on these cloud-based services.

"The global Microsoft outage is a stark reminder of how heavily we rely on a handful of big tech providers," said Rob Demain, chief executive of cybersecurity firm e2e-assure.

Matt Saunders, field chief technology officer at tech consultancy Adaptavist, said it would be challenging to mitigate against a similar problem.

"Classic IT disaster planning strategies suggest using a second service for resilience, but in practice, it's almost impossible for a business to function without tools such as Microsoft," he said.

"In the wake of this outage, organisations must have a plan in place for how to work without such tools, for when incidents like this occur."

#105. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#104. AI risks are the biggest threat to the financial system, Fed survey finds

Threats from artificial intelligence topped the list for greatest risks to the financial system, far exceeding any other dangers, according to respondents who participated in a Federal Reserve survey released Friday.

The central bank's twice-yearly look at a variety of dangers saw a surge in respondents saying that the biggest danger comes from AI along several fronts including debt, stock market valuations and security risks.

"Respondents noted three distinct areas of concern: (1) elevated equity valuations and the possibility that lower-than-expected earnings could result in lower valuations and a market correction; (2) cybersecurity risk, stemming from either a malicious attack by sophisticated state actors or damage caused by frontier AI models themselves; and (3) risks associated with complex forms of AI-related financing," the report stated.

The report comes with Wall Street on edge over high-flying AI hyperscalers, while communities are on alert for the rapid spread of data centers used to power the technology. Some 56% of voters in this month's midterm elections said data centers were of crucial or major importance in their choices, according to exit polling.

Respondents to the Fed survey released Friday include a variety of market participants, economists, analysts and institutional sources. The report covers the period up to Oct. 28, just a few days shy of the election.

Of the group, 87% cited AI as a "salient risk" to the system. That was up from 50% in the survey during the spring of this year and well ahead of the next most-cited danger, a risk asset correction, which was mentioned by 52% of those surveyed.

Other leading risks were higher long-term rates (48%), cyberattacks (43%) and fiscal debt sustainability (43%), the latter coming with the U.S. debt over $40 trillion and the budget deficit near $2 trillion.

The report otherwise found the system fairly stable, though it mentioned that hedge fund leverage is at its highest since 2013 and concentrated on a relatively small group.
